5. Named, explicitly, the one real external dependency this surfaced: the `SYNOPTIC_TOKEN` credential is not available in this environment (confirmed via the actual live error, `.env` left unread per this project's own deny rule) — this blocks the anchor-observation fetch specifically, nothing else. With that one call substituted by a realistic fixture, the rest of the pipeline (forecast fetch — genuinely live — feature construction, prediction, logging) was verified end to end with **no fallback, requested method == actual method == `ml_wind_corrected`**, for both corridors; log entries inspected directly show all four distinct timestamps, model version, and input provenance populated correctly.
6. Added 7 new regression tests (`backend/tests/forecast_shadow_pipeline_test.py`): anchor-fetch success/unknown-city/failed-fetch/empty-response, feature-frame enrichment completeness, and positive/negative controls for the full wind-corrected path (succeeds with no fallback when the anchor source works; falls back explicitly, naming `SYNOPTIC_TOKEN`, when it doesn't).

### Session: 2026-10-06 (continued), conducted by Aaron Phan — wire the shadow pipeline to the real weather-provider composition

The user asked why the shadow module's live anchor fetch only ever used Synoptic when the project
has five weather providers (TCEQ CAMS, ASOS 1-minute, NSRDB, Open-Meteo Archive, Synoptic). Traced
it to `forecast_shadow_pipeline.py:293`'s hardcoded `provider="synoptic"` call -- bypassing the
project's own ratified composition (`weather_provider.py`: TCEQ primary for wind/temp/RH, with
`fetch_weather`'s own `fallback=True` automatically retrying Synoptic on a primary failure) entirely,
rather than a deliberate decision to skip it.

Fixed by calling the real composition correctly: `fetch_live_anchor_observation` now requests
`provider="tceq"` with the correct CAMS site ID (`config.TCEQ_STATIONS[city]`), not a Synoptic
airport code. Traced and resolved one real wrinkle first: ASOS 1-minute supplementing is wired only
into the Synoptic branch of `_fetch_station` (confirmed in `weather_provider.py`'s own docstring --
"not in WX-01's scope" for TCEQ), so a TCEQ-primary success never gets ASOS's finer resolution; for
this function's single most-recent-reading lookup (not a bulk historical fetch), TCEQ's own hourly
cadence is sufficient and this asymmetry is documented as by-design, not an oversight.

**Found and closed a second real gap while verifying live:** `fetch_weather`'s own automatic
fallback only fires on a RAISED exception or a wholly empty response -- it has no way to know this
caller only cares about one specific row (the latest, at-or-before-decision_time). Confirmed
empirically this session: TCEQ's `daily_summary.pl` returns a full 24-local-hour skeleton for the
current day with trailing NaN rows for hours not yet reported -- a MECHANICALLY SUCCESSFUL response
whose latest row can still be unusable. Closed by refactoring `fetch_live_anchor_observation` into
an explicit two-attempt chain: TCEQ primary, then (only if TCEQ's latest row is unusable, not just
on a raised exception) an explicit Synoptic retry at the same station-resolution `weather_provider`
already uses internally -- reusing the established null-wind-field fallback check rather than adding
a new policy. Both attempts are logged by name; the final log/provenance records the ACTUAL provider
that supplied each value (`wind_source`, cross-checked against which attempt succeeded), never just
whichever was requested.

**Verified live, for real, both corridors, after the fix:**
- `AUSTIN_CORPUS_CHRISTI`: **genuine, fully-live, no-fallback completion** --
  `requested=ml_wind_corrected actual=ml_wind_corrected fallback_used=False`. Both cities' TCEQ
  attempts hit a null trailing hour; both explicit Synoptic retries succeeded (`KAUS`, `KCRP`),
  logged with `anchor_source="synoptic"` per city -- the exact corridor that had never completed its
  real predictive path at any point earlier this session.
- `MIDLAND_EL_PASO`: honest fallback. Midland's station has a genuine null wind reading on BOTH TCEQ
  and its Synoptic retry right now -- confirmed as a real, current station gap, not a code defect,
  by the log naming both attempts explicitly.

Updated 6 existing tests in `backend/tests/forecast_shadow_pipeline_test.py` to mock the real
TCEQ-primary call shape (CAMS IDs, `wind_source` provenance) instead of the old Synoptic-only mocks,
and added one new positive control proving the explicit Synoptic retry fires correctly when TCEQ's
response is mechanically successful but unusable.

**Verified, not merely trusted:** ran `backend/tests/forecast_experiment_audit_test.py` +
`backend/tests/forecast_shadow_pipeline_test.py` + `backend/tests/forecast_shadow_collect_test.py`
together (152/152 pass, 1 new) both before and after, plus two real live invocations of

### Session: 2026-10-06 (continued), conducted by Aaron Phan — adversarial mutation-testing pass on the shadow pipeline

Claude Code was used to run a manual adversarial + mutation testing campaign (the
`adversarial-mutation-test` plugin skill's methodology, adapted: no `nix`/`mutation-probe` binary
available in this environment, so each mutation was applied by hand -- edit, run the targeted test
file, observe pass/fail, revert immediately -- rather than via the skill's bundled tool) against the
four shadow-prototype modules: `forecast_shadow_pipeline.py`, `forecast_shadow_demo.py`,
`forecast_shadow_collect.py`, `forecast_shadow_healthcheck.py`.

28 manual mutation probes applied across the four modules (conditionals negated, comparisons
flipped, boundaries shifted by one, side-effects deleted, argument orders swapped, constants
changed). 18 survived the pre-existing suite; all 18 closed with new, discriminating tests (each
re-probed individually afterward to confirm the specific mutation that survived is now killed, with
no regression to any other test). No real bug was found that needed a code fix -- every survivor was
a genuine coverage gap in otherwise-correct code, confirmed against each function's own documented
intent before writing a test, never the other direction (an already-buggy behavior encoded as
"expected").

The most significant gap: `forecast_shadow_demo.py` had ZERO dedicated tests before this pass --
only ever exercised by real live-network runs in prior passes, or as a black box by
`forecast_shadow_collect_test.py`'s monkeypatching of `run_once` itself (which never exercises
`run_once`'s own internal logic). A new `forecast_shadow_demo_test.py` closes this, including the
valid-time 6-hour-grid-selection loop -- a piece of logic the module's own comment says was fixed
after a real live run surfaced a bug no synthetic test could have caught, yet had never itself been
tested.

Two other high-value, non-obvious gaps: (1) every pre-existing test supplied exactly ONE candidate
row per fetch, so the "pick the chronologically latest row" selection logic in both
`fetch_live_anchor_observation`'s `_latest_usable_row` and (by the same class of gap)
`forecast_shadow_healthcheck.py`'s `_last_entry` had never actually been exercised with multiple
rows/log lines -- a sort-order or line-selection bug in either would have gone undetected
indefinitely. (2) `WindCorrectionShadowMethod.predict`'s `frame.attrs.update(enriched.attrs)` line
-- which the code's own comment names as fixing a real alias/copy propagation bug -- had no test
asserting on the ORIGINAL frame object's `.attrs` after `predict()` returned; deleting the line
entirely left the suite green.

One mutation (the grid-selection while loop's `<=` boundary in `forecast_shadow_demo.py`) is
accepted as a documented, practically-unreachable edge case rather than forced into a test: it only
differs from correct behavior at an exact nanosecond equality that real wall-clock time essentially
never produces, and the loop is self-correcting regardless of its starting offset (a separate
probed mutation on the initial `+12h` seed confirmed this empirically -- changing it to `+6h`
produces the same final answer, just one more loop iteration).

**Verified, not merely trusted:** every one of the 18 closed gaps was re-probed individually after
its test was added, confirming that SPECIFIC mutation (and no broader set of tests) is what now
fails under it. Two tests were caught and fixed mid-pass when they initially raced real wall-clock
time non-deterministically (an "exactly at the boundary" assertion that can't be reliably hit via
real elapsed time) -- both rewritten to pin the clock via monkeypatch instead of asserting on an
unreproducible timing coincidence. Final counts: `forecast_shadow_pipeline_test.py` 29 -> 43,
`forecast_shadow_healthcheck_test.py` 4 -> 7, `forecast_shadow_demo_test.py` 0 -> 5 (new file),
`forecast_shadow_collect_test.py` unchanged at 4 (no survivors found). Full targeted suite: 37 -> 59
passed. Broader `forecast_experiment_audit_test.py` suite: 120 passed, unchanged, confirming no
regression outside the shadow module.

No GitHub issue was filed (no `audit`/`adversarial` label convention exists in this repo, and this
session has an outstanding unpushed commit by the user's own choice -- filing issues on a
not-yet-pushed state was out of scope). Nothing was pushed to the remote.

### Session: 2026-10-06 (continued), conducted by Aaron Phan — loop: diagnose and fix the GitHub Actions workflow's first real run

A `/loop` agent (scope pre-authorized by the user, narrowly, after an auto-mode classifier
correctly blocked an initial broader attempt) checked the Shadow Collection workflow's first
real run and found it failed with `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'requests'`.

Root cause: `backend/requirements.txt`'s own docstring states it is "the single source of truth
for this repo's runtime dependencies," existing specifically to serve DS-NF2 (reproducibility) --
but `requests` (imported by `station_explorer.py`, reached transitively through
`forecast_shadow_pipeline.py -> corridor_discretization.py -> station_explorer.py`) was never
declared there. It only ever worked locally because `requests` happened to already be installed
in this developer's own `.venv` by some other means, unrelated to `requirements.txt` itself --
exactly the undeclared-dependency failure mode DS-NF2 exists to prevent, surfaced for the first
time by a genuinely clean environment (GitHub's runner) rather than caught locally.

Fixing it required editing `requirements.txt`, which was outside this loop's original
pre-authorized file list. Stopped and asked the user before touching it, per the loop's own
scoping rule, rather than working around the restriction (e.g. patching only inside the workflow
YAML and leaving the real dependency list incomplete). User expanded the authorized scope to
include `requirements.txt` explicitly.

While verifying the fix with a genuinely clean, disposable virtualenv (not just this developer's
own `.venv`, which already had the missing packages installed) rather than trusting the local
test suite's pass, found a SECOND undeclared dependency of the same kind: `matplotlib`, imported
at module level by `backend/dlr_pipeline.py:25-26` and reached via `weather_provider.py`'s own
import of `dlr_pipeline` for `fetch_timeseries` -- same root cause, same blast radius, not yet
triggered because the first missing import (`requests`) had already crashed the run before
reaching it. Added both to `requirements.txt`, pinned to the versions already verified working
(`requests==2.32.5`, `matplotlib==3.10.8`), matching this file's own stated convention of
recording versions already verified in the environment rather than choosing from a registry.

**Verified, not merely trusted:** built a disposable virtualenv from scratch, installed ONLY
`requirements.txt` (nothing else), confirmed `import forecast_shadow_collect` succeeds and a real
end-to-end collector run completes with exit code 0 for both corridors -- not just that the local
test suite still passes, which would not have caught this class of bug at all. Ran
`backend/tests/forecast_shadow_pipeline_test.py` + `forecast_shadow_collect_test.py` +
`forecast_shadow_healthcheck_test.py` + `forecast_shadow_demo_test.py` +
`forecast_experiment_audit_test.py` together (179/179 pass, unchanged).

### Session: 2026-10-06 (continued), conducted by Aaron Phan — loop: fix a real fallback-signal bug found by the first clean run

The same successful GitHub Actions run (after the dependency fix above) surfaced a second, more
interesting issue: with no `SYNOPTIC_TOKEN` secret set yet AND no persisted model artifact
available on the runner (`data/shadow/models/` is git-ignored, local-only), both corridors
printed `requested=ml_wind_corrected actual=raw_forecast fallback_used=False` -- a direct
contradiction. A missing model IS a fallback, but `backend/forecast_shadow_demo.py`'s own
`run_once` caught `FileNotFoundError` and silently swapped `primary` to `RawForecastShadowMethod`
*before* constructing `ShadowPipeline`, so the pipeline itself never saw a substitution happen and
correctly reported `fallback_used=False` from its own narrow point of view -- exactly the kind of
fallback-signal conflation this project has spent several passes eliminating elsewhere (provider
fallback vs. prediction-method fallback kept deliberately distinct).

Worse: this exact behavior was already pinned as *correct* by a test the adversarial-mutation-
testing pass (two commits prior, `2b86c3b`) had added or left in place --
`assert prediction.fallback_used is False  # the demo-level fallback, not ShadowPipeline's own`.
A textbook case of the mutation-testing skill's own stated risk: "a killed mutant is still in
scope... a mismatch means the test enshrines a bug." The bug was real; the coverage was not
protective.

Fixed in `forecast_shadow_demo.py`: track whether a model-missing substitution happened locally,
and if so, use `dataclasses.replace` to correct `prediction.fallback_used`/`fallback_reason`
*before* `pipeline.log_prediction` writes it -- so both the printed line and the persisted JSONL
entry are honest, not just one or the other. Updated the test that had pinned the old behavior to
assert the corrected one, with a comment explaining why the old assertion was wrong rather than
silently flipping it.

Flagged, not silently decided: this run also revealed that the GitHub Actions runner can **never**
produce a real `ml_wind_corrected` prediction today, independent of the `SYNOPTIC_TOKEN` secret --
the trained model artifact itself was never made available to that environment (it only exists in
this developer's local, git-ignored `data/shadow/models/`). Whether to commit model artifacts to
the repo, retrain on first CI run, or something else is a real design decision with tradeoffs,
reported to the user rather than resolved unilaterally.

**Verified, not merely trusted:** ran the full targeted suite (179/179 pass) after the fix, then
re-triggered the actual GitHub Actions workflow and confirmed via its real log output that both
corridors now correctly report `fallback_used=True` with a precise reason, rather than trusting
the local fix alone.

### Session: 2026-10-06 (continued), conducted by Aaron Phan — fix per-corridor isolation: forecast-fetch failures were escaping to a hard crash

After the model-artifact fix, running the workflow repeatedly to confirm `MIDLAND_EL_PASO`'s own
success surfaced a more important finding than the one being checked for: across 5 real runs, a
network timeout hit TWO DIFFERENT providers at different times -- Open-Meteo's single-runs API
(twice, Midland) and separately TCEQ's `daily_summary.pl` (once, Austin's own anchor fetch, which
WAS caught gracefully) -- ruling out "one corridor is cursed" or "one provider is down" as the
explanation. The real pattern: Austin's TCEQ timeout fell back cleanly
(`fallback_used=True`, exit 0); Midland's Open-Meteo timeout did NOT -- it propagated past
`run_once` entirely and surfaced as `forecast_shadow_collect: hard failure for corridor
'MIDLAND_EL_PASO'`, exit code 1, the whole workflow run marked failed.

Root cause, confirmed by reading the code rather than guessing further: `forecast_shadow_demo.py`'s
`run_once` called `shadow.fetch_live_forecast_for_valid_time(...)` with no try/except around it at
all -- only the later model-loading step was wrapped. `collect_once`'s own comment explicitly
promised otherwise ("the expected fallback path for a missing anchor/model/forecast source is
already caught and logged inside run_once"), naming "forecast" as one of three sources that should
degrade gracefully -- the implementation simply never matched that stated intent for this one path.
A prior pass's ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS entry had claimed per-corridor isolation was "confirmed, not
rebuilt" without ever having exercised a forecast-fetch failure specifically to prove it.

Checked with the user before changing anything (this directly contradicts a previous pass's
verification claim). Fixed by catching `shadow.StaleOrMissingDataError` around the forecast-fetch
call and falling back to this project's own established total-unavailability discipline
(`DLRSafetyMonitor`'s G6 gate: on total failure, report the known-conservative `STATIC_RATINGS`
value, never crash or guess silently) -- per endpoint city, reduced by the weakest-link minimum,
since without a forecast row there is no data to compute even `raw_forecast`'s ampacity from. Two
existing tests that had deliberately used `StaleOrMissingDataError` as a "stop here" sentinel were
updated to use a dedicated sentinel exception instead, since `run_once` now legitimately catches
the real one.

**Verified, not merely trusted:** added a new regression test exercising the exact failure this
fix addresses (a synthetic timeout raised from the forecast-fetch call), asserting the fallback
method name, the correct weakest-link static-rating value (MIDLAND/EL_PASO -> 1450.0A, the lower
of the two), and that the persisted log entry is honest about it. Ran the full targeted suite
(180/180 pass, 1 new) before committing.

### Session: 2026-10-07, conducted by Aaron Phan — mitigate the repeated Midland retrieval timeout

Triggered a fresh run to verify both corridors' retrieval end to end. Austin succeeded genuinely;
Midland failed identically to four prior runs -- same Open-Meteo single-runs API timeout, same
stale-looking run timestamp. Investigated rather than retrying blindly or assuming a transient
blip, per the user's explicit direction.

Ruled out two real possibilities before settling on a third: (1) the exact real request (full
params -- units, model, timezone, forecast_days, read directly from
`open_meteo_forecast_provider.py`) was reconstructed and run directly from this machine, twice,
for both Midland and Austin -- both succeeded in under a second, ruling out a broken request,
an unavailable run, or an Open-Meteo-side outage for this specific query; (2) the request
parameters, coordinates, and code path were otherwise identical to what Austin's (working) call
uses. What remained, consistent with every run observed: Midland is always the SECOND corridor
processed, with zero delay after Austin's request, in `forecast_shadow_collect.py`'s own loop --
pointing at a per-source-IP rate limit on Open-Meteo's side, triggered specifically by GitHub
Actions' shared runner IP pool serving many unrelated jobs' requests in the same window.

Added `INTER_CORRIDOR_DELAY_S` (15s) to `forecast_shadow_collect.py`, applied only BETWEEN
corridors (never before the first, since there is no prior request to space apart from). Framed
explicitly in its own docstring as a mitigation for a working hypothesis, not a confirmed root-
cause fix -- if corridors still fail after this, the rate-limit theory itself needs revisiting,
not just a longer delay.

**Verified, not merely trusted:** added a dedicated test proving the delay fires between
corridors and not before the first; added an autouse fixture pinning the delay to 0 in every
other existing test, since without it those tests would now sleep for real wall-clock seconds per
corridor for no reason. Ran the full targeted suite (181/181 pass, 1 new) before committing.

### Session: 2026-10-07 (continued), conducted by Aaron Phan — diagnostic order-swap confirms position-dependence, 15s delay insufficient

Verified the 15s delay's actual effect rather than assuming it worked: triggered a run, got the
exact same Midland timeout as every prior run, unchanged. Investigated further per the user's
explicit direction rather than lengthening the delay blindly.

Made one clearly-labeled TEMPORARY diagnostic commit (`4a5aeb8`) reversing corridor processing
order for a single test run, to distinguish "always whichever corridor is second" from "always
Midland specifically." Result: with Midland first and Austin second, MIDLAND succeeded genuinely
and AUSTIN failed with the identical Open-Meteo timeout -- conclusive proof this is purely
position-dependent (always the second request in the loop), never corridor- or location-specific.
Reverted the diagnostic change immediately as promised, regardless of the result, and updated
`INTER_CORRIDOR_DELAY_S`'s own docstring to record the measured finding (15s confirmed
insufficient, not just "untested") rather than leaving a stale, superseded rationale in the code.

**Verified, not merely trusted:** re-ran the full targeted shadow-module suite after reverting the
diagnostic change (61/61 pass across the four most relevant test files) to confirm the revert
itself introduced no regression.

### Session: 2026-10-07 (continued), conducted by Aaron Phan — isolate and fix the real root cause: per-process connection reuse

User proposed the decisive test: run each corridor as a fully independent job on its own separate
GitHub Actions runner. Built a temporary diagnostic workflow (`shadow_collect_diagnostic.yml`,
two parallel jobs, no shared process, no delay) to test it directly rather than guessing further.
Result: BOTH corridors succeeded genuinely, every time, with zero failures -- conclusively ruling
out GitHub's shared-runner-IP-pool rate limiting (isolated runners still draw from the same shared
IP pool and would still collide if that were the real cause).

Before that, checked Open-Meteo's actual published rate limits (their public pricing page): free
tier is 600 calls/minute, 5,000/hour, 10,000/day -- so generous that two sequential requests can't
plausibly trigger it; also confirmed no rate-limit header is exposed on a successful response.
This had already weakened the rate-limit theory considerably before the isolated-runner test
settled it outright.

Conclusion: the failure is specific to issuing BOTH requests within the SAME Python process
(`forecast_shadow_collect.py`'s own sequential loop) -- not the network, not either provider, not
GitHub's infrastructure. Most likely mechanism: HTTP keep-alive/connection-pool reuse at a layer
below `requests`'s own per-call `Session` (each `requests.get()` call already opens and closes its
own session, but an underlying kept-alive TCP/TLS connection to the same host can still be reused
across calls). Fixed by sending `Connection: close` on `fetch_single_run`'s request
(`open_meteo_forecast_provider.py`) -- the one function call the shadow pipeline's forecast-fetch
path actually uses, confirmed earlier this session. Framed explicitly, same discipline as every
abandoned theory before it: a mitigation for this specific, now best-supported hypothesis, not an
unconditionally confirmed fix -- if failures persist, this hypothesis needs revisiting too.

Deleted the temporary diagnostic workflow once its question was answered, per its own stated
purpose; it was never meant to be a lasting addition.

**Verified, not merely trusted:** added a dedicated test (`test_fetch_single_run_forces_connection_close`)
pinning that the header is actually sent, not just intended; updated the shared `_mock_get` test
helper to accept and record `headers` (every other existing caller still works unmodified, since
the parameter defaults to `None`). Ran the full relevant suite across six test files (216/216
pass, 1 new) before committing.

### Session: 2026-10-07 (continued), conducted by Aaron Phan — fix silent data-loss bug in collection persistence

While assembling collected results for the user to take to external analysis, checked the
`shadow-collection-data` branch's actual commit history rather than trusting a single read of the
latest commit. Every one of the 11 real collection runs so far produced exactly 1 line per
corridor's `.jsonl` file on that branch -- never more, confirmed by walking every commit's file
content directly. Root cause: the workflow's commit step used `cp data/shadow/predictions/*.jsonl
...`, which replaces the destination file wholesale; since each GitHub Actions run starts from a
fresh checkout with no local copy of prior predictions (by design -- `/data/shadow/predictions/`
is git-ignored on `main`), each run's single new line overwrote, rather than joined, whatever was
previously committed to the data branch. Ten runs' worth of real predictions were silently and
irrecoverably lost before this was caught.

Checked with the user before fixing (a correct call, not an obvious one -- the user could
reasonably have wanted the partial data as-is first). Fixed by replacing the `cp` with a per-file
append loop (`cat "$f" >> destination`), which both creates a nonexistent destination file (first
run, or a brand-new corridor) and correctly appends to an existing one. Explicitly handles the
case where a corridor produced no local file at all (e.g. a crash before `log_prediction` ever
ran) without corrupting what's already on the branch.

**Verified, not merely trusted:** the original overwrite bug was first CONFIRMED (not assumed) by
reading every commit's file content directly, not just the latest one. The fix's three real
cases (branch file absent, branch file present and growing, a corridor producing no file at all)
were each exercised against a disposable temp directory under `bash` specifically (not this
session's interactive `zsh`, whose different glob-expansion behavior on a no-match pattern would
have given a false negative) before trusting it in CI. Ran the full relevant local test suite
(61/61 pass, unchanged -- this is a workflow-YAML-only fix, no Python code touched) as a
regression check.

### Session: 2026-10-07 (continued), conducted by Aaron Phan — render a top-level summary table on the data branch

User asked for a way to see the collected predictions as a table, visible at the top level of the
`shadow-collection-data` branch without digging into raw JSONL files.

Added `backend/forecast_shadow_render_table.py`: a pure function (`render_markdown`) turning the
collector's JSONL logs into a Markdown document, one section per corridor, most-recent-prediction-
first, plus a CLI entry point. Wired into `.github/workflows/shadow_collect.yml`'s existing commit
step -- runs after the append-fix logic (so it picks up this run's newly-appended entries too),
writes to the data branch's own top-level `README.md`, which GitHub renders automatically when
that branch is opened.

Carried forward two things already established earlier this session rather than letting them get
lost in a fresh file: the "experimental/shadow, not field-validated" basis caveat this whole phase
has required on every number, and the just-confirmed finding that `STATIC_RATINGS` is this
project's own unverified placeholder, never confirmed by Power Systems -- both appear at the top
of the rendered table, not just in a document three files away.

Deliberately NOT including a dynamic "rendered at" timestamp in the output: the file must be a
pure function of the JSONL input so a day with no new predictions regenerates byte-identical
content, preserving the workflow's existing `git diff --cached --quiet` no-op-commit behavior
rather than forcing a spurious commit every single run regardless of whether new data landed.

**Verified, not merely trusted:** 8 new unit tests (empty-state caveat text, sorting, pipe-
character escaping inside a table cell, null-value handling, long-reason truncation, and an
explicit determinism check across two renders of the same input). Also ran the renderer against
the REAL data currently on the `shadow-collection-data` branch (fetched via `git show`) to confirm
the output reads correctly against actual collected data, not just synthetic fixtures. Full
relevant suite (224/224 pass, 8 new) before committing.
